**The residue pass that declared this area drained started from every code the ledger calls `kernel-delegated`, and the eighth family had just come from an `erased` row.** That is a denominator hole, not a judgement call: 60 rows carry `erased`, and an erasure justified by the TYPED SURFACE — "the renderer never emits it", "the handle offers no such method" — is a fact about rulekit's builder and says nothing about a stored model, because `rule check` and `modify --condition` both take raw condition text and the portable checker reads stored declarations. Re-reading the 60 with that question produced this family within the hour.

**Most of the seam is already closed or out of scope, which is why naming it matters more than the one family.** Eight of the renderer-erased condition codes are ALREADY checker-reported (`MVK_FIELDNAME_NOT_UNIQUE`, `MVK_TOO_MANY_PARENT_GROUPS`, `MVK_BRACKET_MISSING`, both wildcard classes, `MVK_NO_FIELDS_ALLOWED`, `MVK_NO_WILDCARDS_ALLOWED`); the rest are per-operator operand TYPING or ARITY, the standing gap this plan excludes. `MVK_NO_GROUPS_ALLOWED` was the one member that is neither — an operand KIND law, whose mirror this checker already carries.

**A tenth row was chased and closed on the way, and its ledger reason was wrong.** `MVK_ITERATION_RESTRICTION_IN_A12_MODEL` is recorded as erased because "rulekit renders only the unrestricted star". Its only A12-model raiser is `IterationChecker.checkIterationRestriction4A12Model`, which fires on `IExtmpRule.getRootgroupIterationRestriction()` / `getRowIterationRestriction()` — two getters declared solely on the H-model rule interface, with no DM-JSON spelling anywhere in the kernel. So the code is unreachable from a stored A12 model for a TYPE-level reason that no raw string defeats, and the recorded reason named a construct the gate never reads.

**The family itself is [KF327](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf327)'s trap arriving from the other side.** There, a family built from the operators' names would have refused five legal shapes; here it would have refused eight, because every field-LIST carrier expands a group operand instead of refusing it. Two carriers refuse, and both are the scalars. The discriminating pair is the two wildcard rows, which point opposite ways: starring a repeatable group moves the verdict INTO the kind class, and starring one that does not repeat moves it OUT — so the star is neither noise nor a uniform signal, and the family compares the operand's star count against the number of repeating levels on its path rather than testing for a star at all.

### C record — the eighth relational family: a code the ledger called ERASED, and a two-character over-rejection (2026-09-14)

**`MVK_POINT_IN_A12_MODEL` was carried as erased for the right reason and the wrong scope.** The ledger row said the typed renderer never emits the `.*` path suffix, which is true of the renderer and settles nothing about a model: `rule check` and `modify --condition` both take a raw condition string, so a stored declaration can carry the spelling and A12 duly rejects it. The code had no measurement anywhere in this estate before [KF356](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf356).

**The family's first version over-rejected, which is the one direction this project forbids, and no new test caught it.** `".*" in written` matches the same two characters inside a starred UP-STEP (`..*`), a different construct A12 answers with `MVK_INVALID_WILDCARD_REL`. Two rows of `RepetitionWildcardTest` — a family landed weeks earlier, about a different construct — went red and were the whole signal. The narrowing is the one the resolver's own `withoutRepetitionSuffix()` already draws: the dot must not itself follow a dot.

**It is the second family that deliberately reports where A12 stops.** On `Insured.*/InsuredName` A12 names the suffix class alone, because the suffix gate sits ahead of the wildcard's landing site within the segment. The checker names `MVK_INVALID_WILDCARD` too, and the policy test is the one the combination family already drew: fixing the suffix leaves `Insured*`, still a star on a group that does not repeat, so the second defect is independently real rather than an artefact of the first.

### C record — the seventh relational family, and the set-wide CONSTANT the estate had carried for months (2026-09-14)

**The whole-rule chain read in the sixth record paid again, and this time the finding was in the SET rather than in any row.** Every measurement of parallel iteration in this estate — the accepted cross-group read, the pure-negative rejection, `IterationLawsTest`'s four rows, the new grid's five — pairs two index fields both declared STRING. So "two groups whose index field has the same NAME" and "…the same name and TYPE" were one thing across every row anyone had ever run here, and the record's own prose says "same-name/type" without any row having varied the type. Declaring the second key NUMBER draws `MVK_ERROR_FIELD_INVALID_INDEX_TYPE`, a class with no evidence anywhere, ahead of the iteration gates.

**That single row is what makes the family safe, and without it the family would have been wrong.** A checker pairing groups on the index field's NAME alone reports `MVK_PARALLEL_ITERATION_NOT_ALLOWED` where A12 reports the index-type class — a wrong class on a correct rejection, which is the defect this estate shipped once in September and built three differentials to catch. It was found by asking the set-constant question of the ACCOUNT rather than of a row, which is the one confound no control inside the set can catch.

**A second over-rejection was closed the same way.** A STARRED read of the joint group is ADMITTED — the star puts a wildcard at that level instead of the joint-iteration marker — so a family reading "the condition names two index-keyed groups" would refuse a legal model. Both bounds are in the family's KDoc as measured rows rather than as reasoning.

**The family deliberately asks for LESS than A12 does.** A12 compares the two index fields with `matchBaseType`; this asks for the same declared type string, and for the same field name, which is the intersection of what has been measured. Whether a differing NAME alone stops the joint iteration is unmeasured; both unknowns resolve towards silence.

### C record — the sixth relational family, and the WHOLE-RULE gate chain the estate had never read (2026-09-14)

**One kernel method carries six classes in source order, and reading it is the cheapest survey in this slice.** `ParserService.checkNormalRule` is A12's whole-RULE check chain, running after a condition parses: parallel iteration against `RepetitionNotUnique`, a parallel-iterated filter, a filter that parallel-iterates, a filter using a semantic index, two `RepetitionNotUnique` calls, two of its legacy twin, and a raw-type `Length`. Three of those codes had no measurement here. The family landed from the one that is decidable without modelling iteration — the call COUNT — and the rest of the chain is now a named, ordered candidate list rather than seven ledger rows nobody had connected.

**Eight predictions, eight right, including both precedence rows.** That is the second family in a row where reading A12's firing site first turned the grid into a transcription; the method is now in [TESTING-SPEC](TESTING-SPEC.md)'s landing checklist rather than only in these records.

**The mirror found the one row the grid could not, and it is a wrong-CLASS risk rather than an over-rejection.** The checker's call scanner descends into a `Having` filter body, so a condition with one top-level call and one inside a filter counted as two — and A12 answers that shape with `MVK_DISALLOWED_CONSTRUCT_IN_FILTER_CONDITION`, because the construct is refused in that position outright. Removing the filter's call leaves one legal call, so the count was an artefact rather than a defect. The fix subtracts calls found inside filter clauses, and the row went into the kernel grid so the claim rests on a measurement rather than on the catalog's prose.

**This family breaks the estate's usual silence rule on purpose, which is worth stating because it looks like the defect the others avoid.** Every relational family so far stays silent wherever another class pre-empts it. Here the pre-empting classes belong to CONSTRUCTS, and a condition with two calls and a bad `@From` scope genuinely has both defects — A12 just stops at the first. Suppressing the count would hide work the author still owes; the filter case is different precisely because there the second call is not a defect at all.

**The Java owner's corrective, written long before this grid, states the same law in its own words** — "counts nodes, not key fields and not And/Or nesting; one leaf may carry a composite key". The projection guard forced it into view, which is the second time that guard has doubled as an independent check on a measurement.

### C record — the fifth relational family, and the enumeration that found a whole unmeasured surface (2026-09-14)

**Selected by ranking the residue on EXISTING EVIDENCE rather than on the ledger's prose, which is what surfaced it.** Every `MVK_*` the ledger calls `kernel-delegated` minus every code the checker reports leaves 102; scoring each by how many times it appears in a laws test and in KERNEL-FINDINGS put a cluster of nine semantic-index codes at the very bottom, five of them with zero mentions anywhere in the estate. That is not a thin measurement — it is a construct whose entire diagnostic vocabulary had never been drawn, hiding behind ledger rows that each read as a settled law.

**Reading A12's firing site BEFORE predicting turned the grid from a search into a transcription, and that is the reusable part.** `CheckSemanticIndexImpl.doChecks` is one method carrying the whole chain in source order, so the grid's rows and their order were read off it rather than guessed; the previous families discovered the same ordering one failing prediction at a time. Two of the nine predictions were still wrong, and both were about a gate ABOVE the chain rather than inside it — which is exactly what a source read of one method cannot show.

**Two ledger rows were wrong in the way this plan has now recorded four times: written from the code's NAME.** `MVK_SEMANTIC_INDEX_REF_OP` is never reported at all — A12 passes its bare text as the operation NAME into the shared value-validation check, which raises `MVK_INVALID_RAW_TYPE` — and `MVK_SEMANTIC_INDEX_NO_FIELD` is reachable only when the key does not iterate, because the ordinary iteration gate answers first under a second name. Both rows named real laws under codes that cannot carry them.

**A `grep --include` with unquoted globs produced a confident ZERO, and it nearly retired a live code.** The first enumeration concluded `MVK_SEMANTIC_INDEX_BELOW_WILDCARD` had no raiser in the kernel and was therefore dead prose; the shell had eaten the include patterns, and the code is live from a second site entirely. The record's own rule caught it — re-derive a suspicious zero a second way — and the cost of not doing so would have been a finding asserting a negative existential about somebody else's source.

**The family reports the one cell it can decide without knowing the rule's anchoring.** `SEMANTIC_INDEX_KEY` names `MVK_SEMANTIC_INDEX_NO_FIELD` when the key resolves to a group with nothing repeating on its path. Where something repeats, whether the read iterates turns on where the rule sits, which this checker does not model — so it stays silent and leaves those to A12, an under-report rather than a class it would probably get wrong.

### C record — standing candidate: the single-code rejection claim, which cannot see a second gate (2026-09-14)

**Three measurement defects in two days came from one place, and it has a denominator.** `KernelLaws.rejects*(…, code)` asserts the expected code is AMONG the diagnostics — `KernelLaws` says so itself, which is why the stricter `preemptedCode` spelling exists. A containment claim cannot see a co-firing second gate, cannot establish which gate reports FIRST, and cannot notice that the shape it measures satisfies a second condition nobody named. Re-derive rather than citing these: **203 `rejects*` calls, 223 `accepts*` calls, 28 strict/pre-empted spellings** (2026-09-14), so roughly 175 rejection claims stand on containment alone. `accepts*` carries no exposure — there is no code to hide.

**The three that have already fallen, each found by replacing one file's single assertions with a grid asserting the COMPLETE class list:**

| what was wrong | how the single assertion hid it |
|---|---|
| [KF340](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf340)'s mechanism — "resolution wins" | every row put the bad name first, so text order and resolution order were one thing |
| `SumOfProducts`' repeatable-level clause, short by a conjunct on FOUR surfaces | the one row exercising that arm gave both operands the same owning group |
| [KF349](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf349) — `MVK_NOT_IN_GROUP`'s reachability, which the ledger recorded as unexplained | the one cell sat on an index-keyed fixture, and nothing recorded that this was load-bearing |

**The queue is ranked by DISTINCT rejection codes in a grid-free file, not by assertion count** — `AggregateSlotLawsTest` (8 codes / 12 claims), `IterationLawsTest` (8 / 8), `NumberLawsTest` (6 / 14), `AggregateGroupOperandLawsTest` (6 / 8), `PathLawsTest` (5 / 6), `RepetitionNotUniqueKeyKindLawsTest` (4 / 4); fourteen files assert three or more. *(Corrected 2026-09-14, within the hour: this first ranked by assertion count and sent the reader to `RuleGroupIterationLawsTest` (42 claims) — which is among the most rigorously measured files in the estate, its claims probing ONE gate on single-defect shapes with discriminating cases already built. Precedence can only be unmeasured between codes that a file actually asserts, so the count of DISTINCT codes is the proxy and the count of claims is noise.)* Fifty-two files carry single assertions and no grid at all.

**This is NOT a sweep to run to completion, and saying so is the point.** Converting 175 claims would be a large mechanical job with most of its cells confirming what is already believed; the yield lives where a shape can break TWO rules at once, which is judgment. So it is a standing candidate worked one file at a time, highest-count first, and each pass is cheap: build the grid with the complete class list, write the expectations as PREDICTIONS, and run once — a wrong prediction IS the finding. Every one of the three above was a wrong prediction on the first run.

**First pass on the queue's top item, and it corrects the method: GREP THE ESTATE PER CODE BEFORE PREDICTING (2026-09-14).** `AggregateSlotLawsTest` was worked as two grids. The filter grid paid — both of that construct's other gates pre-empt `MVK_DISALLOWED_CONSTRUCT_IN_FILTER_CONDITION` and neither co-fires, which four containment assertions could not have said, and the four rows collapsed into one grid with two rows added. The operand grid paid **nothing**: its two wrong predictions were both already measured in this repository, one as [KF263](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf263)'s within-model 2×2 (`MVK_DATEFORMATS_NOT_COMPATIBLE` reads the component SET, so two DATE fields differing only in format string are ADMITTED — the code is misnamed) and one as the three-way homogeneity partition the catalog already publishes (`MVK_STRING_ENUM_AND_NON_STRING_ENUM`). It was discarded rather than landed, because a lock restating a law another lock already holds is cost without reach.

**So the ranking measures where CONTAINMENT assertions live, not where knowledge is missing, and those are different things.** A file can assert eight codes by containment while every one of its precedence questions is answered elsewhere — the codes travel across families, so the file that asserts a code is rarely the file that owns its law. The fix costs one command per code and goes *before* the predictions: `git grep -n '<CODE>' -- docs/ rulekit/src/main/resources/catalog/operators.json`, then keep only the rows no existing entry answers. Applied here it would have cut a ten-row grid to two and saved a build. This is the record's own "look first in your own findings" rule, which the queue's ranking quietly encourages you to skip.

### C record — the third and fourth relational families, and the reader capability that had been calling itself a blocker (2026-09-14)

**The blocker was one line of contract, not a missing analysis.** Order 6's cell named "the condition reader cannot reduce `@From` and `Having` clauses to operands" as what the remaining relational families wait on, and the shape parser had been parsing both clauses correctly all along — `ConditionShape` has productions for each. What was missing was only that `callsIn` threw the structure away: `CallOperand.path` is null whenever a clause appears, so every consumer saw an operand it could not compare, and three separate places had written that silence down as a design decision. The fix is additive and leaves `path` exactly as narrow as it was — `base` is the spec before the first clause and `clauses` carries each one with its keyword as spelled, its own reduced path, and its authored text. Cutting only at depth zero is the whole subtlety, since a filter body may carry its own bracketed spec with its own clause.

**A second consumer followed immediately, and it is the one that paid for the mirror rows.** `FILTER_WILDCARD` reports a repetition wildcard inside a `Having` filter (`MVK_NO_WILDCARDS_ALLOWED`), a position the reference-level wildcard family deliberately does not judge because position is what picks the class there. Writing its mirror's DECLINED rows turned up an over-rejection the family had shipped past its own grid: a star whose LEAF resolves to nothing was being reported, and A12 draws `MVK_INVALID_ENTITY` there. The prediction that it would not came from `RepetitionWildcard`'s measured "the wildcard class outruns the entity gate" — true at the NON-repeatable landing site and false here, so that ordering belongs to the landing site rather than to the wildcard check. **A silence row nobody writes is an over-rejection nobody sees**, which is why these mirrors state every declined shape rather than only the reported ones.

**The consumer landed in the same slice, deliberately.** A reader capability with no family reading it is the "parsed but never consumed" class this repository has already shipped once, so `REPETITION_SCOPE_GROUP` — `@From` must name a REPEATABLE group, `MVK_GROUP_NOT_REPEATABLE` — went in beside it. The `:consistency:jvmTest` sweep after the family compiled was green on all 366 other tests, which is the mirror-layer check for an over-rejection no family's own grid can see.

**Two kernel rows had to be measured before the family could report anything, and one of them refuted its prediction.** The repeatability class had only ever been measured where the scope was the ROOT, so reporting from `isRepeatable` alone would have transferred it to a carrier nobody had run — an ordinary non-repeatable group confirms it. And a scope naming a FIELD draws `MVK_NO_FIELDS_ALLOWED`, not the repeatability class, because the KIND gate runs first: "not a repeatable group" is true of every field, so the plausible reading would have attached this family's fix to a different gate on every one of them. **The generalizable part is that both rows were about what the family may NOT say**, and neither would have been asked by someone confirming a law they already believed.

### C record — the second relational family, where the CHECKER found the missing rows twice (2026-09-14)

**Selected in one command, and the checklist made the landing cheap.** The residue script ranks candidates by how narrow their existing evidence is; three of the top six were already covered or need the per-operator catalog, and the root-only error-target pair had a six-cell grid plus machinery `ErrorFieldReference` already owns. Running the new seven-step checklist FIRST established that neither code carries a corrective and neither is named by the kits — so three of the seven steps dropped before a single build ran, where the previous family discovered the same facts one failing guard at a time.

**The measured law needs three conditions and I had grounds for one.** A rule reaching its error field only from ABOVE its own group, while reading a repeating field, draws one of two classes by the read's carrier; pinning turns out to be a conjunction — a reference that reaches the target AND sits at or below the rule's group — and neither half alone gives it. [KF348](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf348) holds the grid across three carriers.

**The two conditions I was missing were CONSTANTS of the grid, and in both cases the estate's existing mirrors found them, not my reading.** Every row carried a reaching reference, so "reached from above" and "reached at all" were one thing — `ErrorFieldReferenceTest` went red on a condition naming a sibling field. Every row put the error field in a repeatable group, so the law read as being about reach when it is also about there BEING a repetition — `FieldVariantFilterTest` went red on an ordinary two-field rule with nothing repeating anywhere. Both were over-rejections, the one direction that is never acceptable, and neither was visible from inside the grid.

**That is the strongest argument yet for the mirror layer, and it is worth stating plainly:** a checker family is measured against the kernel by its own grid and against the REST OF THE ESTATE by every other family's fixtures, and the second catches exactly the confound the first cannot — an unnamed constant — because those fixtures were built for other reasons and share none of it. The two silences are now measured rather than guarded: a flat model is kernel-ADMITTED, and a sibling-only condition draws the referenced-ness class alone.

**A by-product worth keeping.** `FieldVariantFilterTest`'s control rows asserted an empty expectation that had never been put to the kernel — the defect class recorded earlier in this log. Measuring the flat shape to ground this family's guard also confirmed those controls, so one row closed two questions.

### C record — the first RELATIONAL family, and the cell that separated two readings of one pre-emption (2026-09-14)

**It was selected by re-deriving the residue the plan told me to re-derive rather than cite, and the residue's own shape was the answer.** Every `MVK_*` the ledger calls `kernel-delegated` minus every code the checker reports leaves 105; 60 of those already carry kernel evidence, and almost all of that 60 is per-operator typing — comparability, operand kinds, date formats — which is the standing gap this plan does not reproduce. What stands out instead is the iteration cluster, which is Order 6. Two near-misses are worth recording because both read as candidates: `MVK_NAME_RULES_DUPLICATE` is already covered, the checker reporting it as `CK_MODEL_NOT_BUILDABLE` because A12 throws instead of raising the code, so it sits in the residue only as an artefact of keying on MVK codes; and `MVK_MULTIPLE_INDEX_FIELDS` had been re-classified the day before as parallel-iteration rather than a declaration law.

**The family is `RepetitionNotUnique`'s key placement, and the naive law over-reports on two of eight cells.** The collapse was already documented — a key needs a repeatable group strictly between the rule's group and itself, and three different shapes draw the one class. What no row measured is what happens when that is violated AND a key sits outside the rule group. Two readings fitted everything that existed, they disagree at exactly one cell, and the cell draws a THIRD class, `MVK_NOT_IN_RULEGROUP`, which had no evidence anywhere in the estate. [KF347](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf347) holds the grid; the checker stays silent on every pre-empted row, because both replacement classes belong to families that do not exist yet and guessing between them attaches one fix to a defect that has another.

**A surviving mutant, not the grid, found the row the grid could not.** The depth carrier added for "is a NESTED repeatable group enough" put the repeatable group directly above the key, so it could not separate "a repeatable group anywhere between" from "the key's own parent is repeatable" — and a mutant reading only the parent survived. The fix was a second measured row two levels down, not a checker change: the implementation was right and the evidence was not. This is the cheapest instance yet of the rule that a mutant with no control IS the finding.

**The fixture grew twice and the twelve pre-existing rows were re-run unchanged each time**, which is what makes them evidence the extension is inert rather than an assumption that adding a group changes nothing.

**Two mechanical consequences arrived on their own, and both are the estate working.** `CheckFamilyWiringTest` refused a new constant with only a call site until the fixture carried a declaration that triggers it, and `CheckerCorrectiveProjectionGuardTest` refused the landing until the corrective was re-projected. That projection is also an independent check on the measurement: the Java owner's corrective, written long before this grid, states the same boundary law in its own words.

### C record — the mirror-versus-laws gap, measured: the decidable half is not the half that was asked for (2026-09-13)

**The recorded gap asked for something no scan can decide, and measuring that was the whole result.** The gap below wants a mirror compared against the laws test it mirrors. Its direct form — every `rulekit.validate.laws.X` a mirror cites must resolve, and every code the mirror asserts must appear in that cited test — was run over all 62 checker mirrors before any guard was written. It flags **15 files**, and almost all of it is noise: `CK_MODEL_NOT_BUILDABLE` is the checker's OWN verdict and no laws test could carry it, `ACCEPTED` and `MODEL_ROOT` are verdict tokens and fixture names, and a mirror legitimately asserts a code measured by a laws test it does not cite. Eleven further mirrors cite no laws test at all because they are infrastructure — wiring, preparation, report shape — with no kernel law to mirror, so the guard would need an exemption list that is exactly the authored denominator this estate refuses. **A guard that fails honest content gets disabled**, so the direct form is recorded as infeasible rather than shipped noisy.

**Moving the same question from the TEST side to the MAIN-SOURCE side makes it decidable, and sharper.** What a mirror asserts is second-hand; what the checker REPORTS is the claim itself — a `Finding` carrying `MVK_X` says *A12 draws this*, which is a statement about the kernel. So the invariant is: every A12 code the checker reports is named by some laws test. Both denominators are derived, there is no exemption list, and it runs at **zero** false positives on the live estate — all 66 reported codes are measured (`CheckerCodeBasisGuardTest`, on `leakGuardTest`; both arms mutation-proven, the floor arm against a scan matching nothing).

**It is weaker than the gap asked for, and the difference is stated rather than glossed.** It would NOT have caught `FieldVariantFilterTest` contradicting [KF305](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf305): the code that mirror over-asserted was measured elsewhere, so a "has some measurement" test passes it. That residue is the scope-transfer class — a code measured on one carrier and reported on another — which no scan can decide and which the witness-grid discipline owns. The gap below is therefore narrowed, not closed.

**The same scan found a live defect in a doc that had been corrected the day before.** [MVK-LEDGER](MVK-LEDGER.md)'s codegen-tier scope note read "five of the 26 are now measured, one consumer reports four"; the derived figures are **nine** and **seven**. Three of the four it missed had been measured before the 2026-09-12 amendment that restated the count. The mechanism is the one CLAUDE.md names as the strengthening edit: each pass INCREMENTED the count by what that pass had just measured, rather than re-deriving it, and a count assembled that way can only ever see the families that think of themselves as belonging to the tier — an element name's alphabet does not. The row is amended in place with the derivation named.

**And `scripts/mutant.sh` could not mutate a file that had just been created.** Its landing proof was `git diff --quiet`, which is silent for an UNTRACKED file, so planting the positive control into the new guard class reported "the file is unchanged after the edit" and restored. It now compares BYTES against the backup it already holds — no index, and the same copy `restore` puts back, so the proof cannot disagree with what will be undone.

### C record — the defect-pairing pass, priced and started: three members, three different shapes (2026-09-13)

**The standing candidate below is no longer a candidate — it is paying, and the price is one grid per member.** Pairing defects within a member has now been run on three of them and produced three genuinely different answers, none of which could be guessed from another: a condition reports the FIRST defect in its text ([KF342](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf342)), a field type reports ALL of them ([KF341](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf341)), and a message LAYERS them — a character gate that wins wherever the character sits, and past it every parameter defect in order ([KF343](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf343)). Three members, three shapes, so the fourth cannot be inferred either.

**Each pass cost one grid and found one live over-report.** The message pass found the checker naming a parameter class beside a control character, where A12 names the character class alone — the forbidden direction, and invisible to every test the three families had, because each family only ever saw a text defective in its own way.

**Amended 2026-09-13 — the fourth member, and the first pass to find a MISSING class rather than an extra one.** An enumeration's value list reports every defect, like a field type ([KF344](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf344)) — but the pairing rows turned up `MVK_CATEGORY_VALUE_DUPLICATE`, which A12 draws for a repeated INTERNAL value while a category exists and which the checker did not emit at all. Read off its name it would have been implemented backwards: a category whose own tokens repeat is ADMITTED. So the pass finds over-reports and under-reports alike, and a class named for the member it is reported against is not evidence about what triggers it.

**Two things the passes have taught that are cheaper than the grid itself.** Measure the pair in BOTH orders: the reversed row is what separates "this class wins" from "the earlier defect wins", and its absence is what made [KF340](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf340) wrong. And measure the GRANULARITY rather than carrying it over: the condition gate is per declaration and the message gate is per message, so a checker that assumed either would have been wrong about the other — one row each, and both were nearly assumed.

**Amended 2026-09-13 — the fifth member, and a second missing class.** A group's two ordering members both report ([KF345](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf345)), and the pairing rows turned up `MVK_SORT_FIELD_DUPLICATE`, which the checker did not emit. The sort list also draws its type class ONCE however many entries are opaque, which the checker does not match — kept deliberately, because it is the count divergence [KF340](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf340) already settled rather than a class one, and a finding per entry names the field to fix.

**The tier trap is real and the grid has to be built around it.** An unresolvable ordering name stops the model BUILDING, so a row putting one opposite a type defect varies buildability rather than the member; those rows live in a test of their own and say so. That is the same "a control that moves two variables is not a control" rule, met in a member where it is easy to miss because both defects are spelled the same way.

**Amended 2026-09-13 — the sixth member closes the list, and the pass ends where it started: on a gap someone had disclosed rather than closed.** A rule's slots report in full ([KF346](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf346)), and the pairing grid ran into `RULE_HAS_NO_ERRORTEXT` — a class the checker deliberately withheld because "which shapes split that way is not measured", written as an honest disclosure and left standing. Enumerating the shapes took six rows and turned the disclosure into a rule with exactly one exception; the class is emitted now, and leaving it out had been an under-report on every shape but one.

**Two traps this member sprang that the earlier five had not.** The rule derived on a ONE-locale model is false on a two-locale one — whitespace suppresses the class only when every declared locale is answered that way — so the first implementation was measured wrong within minutes of being written. And the first version emitted the class wherever no locale was covered, including models with no usable declared locale at all, where A12 reaches no verdict; that is the checker stating its own reading as A12's, caught only because four existing mirror rows went red.

**The pass is complete: six members, six grids.** Three report everything (field type, enum values, group ordering, rule slots), one reports the first defect in its text (a condition), one layers a gate over a full report (a message). No member's answer could be predicted from another's, and every one of the six turned up something live.

### C record — the withholding's mechanism was WRONG, and the set that hid it was my own (2026-09-13)

**The previous record read the order as "reference resolution runs first and wins". It does not — it wins when it comes FIRST in the text** ([KF342](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf342)). Every pair the earlier grid held put the bad name at the head of the condition, so both accounts predicted all of them identically; one row with the position reversed separates them. This is the estate's own named confound — one constant across every row, invisible from inside the set — and it reached a shipped gate and three documents before the next slice's denominator work turned it up.

**The correction cost was small; the miss was not.** Naming only three syntax codes left EIGHT more condition-text classes live, each an over-rejection on a condition that also carries a bad name — the forbidden direction, shipped. They were found by enumerating what reads the condition text rather than by reasoning about the mechanism, which is why the denominator question is worth asking even when the account feels settled.

**A second gate came out of it, and it is shaped differently from every other one here.** `MVK_ERROR_FIELD_NOT_REFERENCED` and `MVK_ERROR_INVALID_LANGUAGE_FEATURE` are withheld by ANY other defect in the same text, wherever it sits, so their trigger is not a property of the model but of what the other families already said. That gate therefore reads the FINDINGS, not the model — the first one here that does.

**The under-report is deliberate and disclosed.** The gate is position-blind because a finding carries no offset into the condition text; threading one through eleven families is a larger change than the over-rejection it buys back, and the cost is naming the bad name where A12 names the other defect — one true thing either way. Recorded here rather than as a gap row because it is a bounded, stated divergence, not open work.

### C record — where the withholding STOPS, and the method blind spot it exposed (2026-09-13)

**A field type reports every defect it carries** ([KF341](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf341)) — the opposite of the two members above, measured hours apart on the same kernel. The stop is a property of particular members, so it is never assumed from a neighbour; the row exists so a later reader cannot generalise the withholding into silence on input A12 answers in full. Nothing needed changing in the checker, which is the point of measuring it.

**The real yield is a blind spot in the METHOD, not a defect in the code.** That row also produced a class neither defect draws alone — `noValueValidation` beside a `pattern`. Every other grid in this estate varies ONE defect at a time, so a combination-only class is structurally unreachable: not tried and found empty, never built. The three withholdings found today were all found the same way, by breaking two things at once.

**What follows is a standing candidate, not a claim:** the single-defect grids are the estate's dominant shape, and each one is a place where a stop or a combination-only class could be hiding. Worth a pass that pairs defects within each member, priced before it is started.

### C record — the THIRD withholding, inside a single member, and what the class is really about (2026-09-13)

**The two withholdings above were between members; this one is inside one.** A condition's unresolvable name withholds every syntax class over the same text ([KF340](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf340)). So the pattern is not "A12 stops between members" but "A12 examines in a fixed order and reports the first thing it finds", at every granularity looked at so far. *(Corrected 2026-09-13 by the record above: the order inside a condition is the TEXT's, not resolution-before-syntax, and the gate this record describes named three of the eleven codes it needed.)*

**Family-per-defect-kind is the design that meets this head on.** Eleven families read one condition text. The checker had no test at all for a text carrying two defects, so nothing was red — and the input is not exotic, it is what a half-written condition looks like.

**Recorded as the codes to DROP, not the codes to keep.** A family added later that reports a new syntax class is then merely an under-report until its code is listed; a keep-list would silently start dropping it the day it landed. The error-target gate opposite is a keep-list for the mirror-image reason, and the asymmetry is deliberate.

**What is still unexamined is the rest of the order.** Three withholdings have been found by asking, each time, "what else does A12 decline to say once this fails?" — and each was found by breaking two things at once, which no single-defect grid can see. The estate's grids are overwhelmingly single-defect.

### C record — the SECOND withholding, and a mirror that had contradicted its own laws test (2026-09-13)

**The sweep the previous record asked for found a wider one.** A rule whose error TARGET does not resolve draws that pair and nothing else — the condition's class and both error-text classes are withheld ([KF339](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf339)). So the first withholding was not a fact about error texts; A12 examines a declaration in ORDER and stops, and the error-text case was one instance.

**It is applied where the report is assembled, not inside a family.** A family-local version would have to be repeated into every family added later, and the one that forgot would over-reject silently — which is exactly how this defect survived. The trade is a blunt instrument: every finding on such a declaration is withheld but the target's own, which is conservative where the measurement stops.

**A checker mirror had asserted the union of both codes and sat green, contradicting its own laws test.** `FieldVariantFilterTest` expected `MVK_ERROR_FIELD_MISSING` **and** `MVK_INVALID_ENTITY` with the comment "which is what A12 does"; [KF305](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf305)'s grid had measured that very row as the target pair alone and said so in ITS comment. Nothing in the estate compares a mirror against the laws test it mirrors, so two contradictory claims about one input were both green for as long as the mirror existed. That gap is worth a guard and is not one this slice adds. *(Narrowed 2026-09-13 — see the mirror-versus-laws record at the head of this log: the direct form proved undecidable without an authored exemption list, and the decidable half that shipped, `CheckerCodeBasisGuardTest`, would not have caught THIS mirror, because the code it over-asserted was measured elsewhere. What remains open is the scope-transfer residue.)*

**The scope question stayed open on purpose.** Whether the withholding reaches non-member defects was probed with an illegal rule name, which makes the model unbuildable — two variables — so it decides nothing and is recorded as a probe rather than a row.

### C record — the cross-member withholding, and the over-rejection three families had been shipping (2026-09-13)

**Found by reading the vocabulary rather than by a failing test.** `FehlertextErrorType.INVALID_CONDITION` says a parameter can only be checked where the condition is valid — so the question was whether the checker reports a parameter class on a model whose condition is broken. It did, and A12 withholds that class entirely ([KF338](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf338)).

**A family-per-member checker gets this wrong by construction.** Every family reads its own member and reports what it finds; nothing in that design expresses "A12 stops asking once a sibling member fails". The fix is a withholding rather than a new family, and it is the first cross-member dependency the estate carries.

**The sweep found the family shipped an hour earlier carrying the same defect.** The character law needs nothing from the condition, so there was no reason to expect it to be gated — and it is. Declaring the parameter fix done without running that row would have left a live over-rejection in a family whose own commit message claimed the over-rejection direction was what it was built to avoid. One shared gate now serves both, and any future family reading `errorMessage` inherits it.

**The shape is not exotic, which is what makes it worth the slice.** A half-typed condition is the ordinary state of a model being edited, and that is exactly when a checker runs. An over-rejection that only appears on malformed input is still an over-rejection on the input a checker most often sees.

**The parse half is deliberately under-covered.** This layer holds no grammar, so "does not parse" is stood in for by an unbalanced delimiter — a necessary condition. Balanced-but-refused text keeps its parameters judged, which can only miss a finding. `incompleteInput` was the first instrument reached for and is the wrong one: it fires only where delimiters ARE balanced, by its own design.

### C record — the forty-first family: the message text's control characters, the cheapest law on the surface (2026-09-13)

**The second pick off the unprefixed re-derivation, and it cost one run.** `INVALID_LINEBREAK` and `INVALID_CHARACTERS` are a character predicate over one string — no resolution, no model walk — and nine rows measured the whole law including both ends of the boundary. It is the cheapest family the checker has, and it was invisible for the same reason the rest of `FehlertextErrorType` was.

**The implementation is the ORDER, not the predicate.** A text carrying both a line break and a tab draws the line-break class ALONE. A reader reporting each kind it finds would over-report on exactly the text an author produces by pasting — the most likely real input on this surface, which is what makes the row worth its run rather than a completeness exercise.

**A repertoire row measured nearby answers a different question, and would have read as coverage.** `RuleMessageAdmissionGateLawsTest` already measured "which characters may a message carry" and found a CODELESS refusal at the BMP boundary. Every row there is printable; nothing in it reaches a control character, and its refusal carries no class at all. A grep for the topic would have returned it and it would have looked like the answer — the narrow-prior-art shape, met here in the estate's own record rather than in a peer's.

### C record — the fortieth slice: the message parameter's ADDRESS, and what the MVK-keyed enumeration could not see (2026-09-13)

**Selected by re-deriving the candidates against the UNPREFIXED vocabulary**, which the record below said had to happen. Reading `FehlertextErrorType` directly turned up six classes and a search of the whole estate — docs, tests, checker — found five of them mentioned NOWHERE: `INVALID_LINEBREAK`, `INVALID_CHARACTERS`, `ERROR_TEXT_FIELDNAME_NOT_UNIQUE`, `INVALID_PATH_SPEC`, `INVALID_CONDITION`. The enumeration blind spot was not a near miss; it hid a whole surface's diagnostic vocabulary.

**It widened the thirty-ninth family rather than minting a fortieth**, because it is the same mechanism one step earlier: resolve the parameter's address, then judge membership. The address had been silently discarded, so three outcomes A12 answers went unreported — and the pick was made on the suspicion that the silence was instead an over-REPORT, since a checker resolving a bare name through a model-wide fallback would pick a candidate and judge membership against it. It was not: the resolver already returns `Reference.Ambiguous` and the family fell silent. The suspicion was still what found the three under-reports, which is the argument for chasing a possible over-rejection even when it turns out not to exist.

**The grid needed a THIRD kind of duplicate to say anything.** A name duplicated in a group the rule declares in is admitted, and so is the same name read from the other carrier — the local hit explains both. Only a name the declaring group does NOT own reaches the model-wide tier, and only a duplicated one of those is refused. A fixture with the first kind alone measures a local-first rule and a two-tier rule identically.

**The checker's fixture was missing the flag the law depends on.** `fieldRefByShortNameAllowed` gates the second tier; the kernel fixture is built by `DocumentModelDraft` and carries it, the checker's hand-written JSON did not, so the mirror reported the unresolvable class where A12 reported the ambiguous one. A hand-built mirror of a drafted fixture silently differs in every default the draft supplies.

**The over-rejection the slice created, and the grid that caught it.** Reporting unresolvable addresses made `$BaseYear$` — a plain name that resolves to nothing — report `INVALID_ENTITY`, where A12 ADMITS it as a message-grammar terminal. The family's "forms this family does not read" grid failed on it immediately. That grid exists to keep disclosed silence honest; here it worked as a regression guard against a widening, which is the stronger job. The fix is the one exclusion this surface DOES justify — and note it is the exact shape removed two commits earlier as speculative: excluding CONDITION keywords was unmeasured and hid findings, excluding the MESSAGE grammar's terminals is measured and prevents an over-rejection. The lesson is not "exclusions are bad" but that an exclusion is worth exactly its measurement.

### C record — the thirty-ninth family: what an error message may name (2026-09-13)

**The first family reporting a class with no `MVK_` prefix**, and the one the record above predicted: `INVALID_FIELD` was invisible to the candidate enumeration because the ledger is keyed by `MVK_*` codes only. The kernel half was already measured by `RuleMessageParameterLawsTest`; what was missing was the boundary an implementation needs, which is now [KF335](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf335).

**The slice was scoped BEFORE it was opened, and the scope is most of the work.** A12's message grammar is a third DSL — a category suffix, a semantic-index key, a row specifier, a base-year terminal with a signed offset, its own quoting rule — and each form this reader did not recognise would have been reported as the membership failure, which is an over-rejection. So it reads the plain `$Name$` / `$Name.value$` form and yields nothing for everything else, with the silence locked by its own grid rather than left as an assumption.

**Three shapes that look exactly like the failure are a different class each.** A name resolving to nothing is `INVALID_ENTITY`; an unterminated parameter is `WRONG_NUMBER_OF_DOLLARS`; and a GROUP draws the membership class rather than `INVALID_GROUP`, so keying the class on the resolved element's kind is wrong in the other direction too. None of the three is guessable.

**A computation's message is gated as tightly as a rule's, by its own members** — its computed target plus what its preconditions and operations read. That was the reverse of the first two readings taken, both off a fixture that never landed: the first injected the computation with a regex that matched nothing, so four ADMITTED rows were measured against an unmodified model; the second gave the computation an operation reading its own target, so its control drew the self-read class. A positive control inside the grid — one row A12 must refuse — is what caught both, and it is why the control sits in the grid rather than beside it.

**One question turned out to be unaskable, which is a result and not a gap.** Separating "the condition reads it" from "it is the rule's error target" cannot be done: A12 refuses a rule whose error field its condition does not name, so the target is necessarily inside the membership set and there is no second term for an implementation to find.

### The candidate ENUMERATION had a blind spot, and it is the selection method rather than any one family (2026-09-13)

**Families 36–38 were all chosen from one diff: the ledger's `kernel-delegated` rows minus the codes the checker's main source emits.** That diff is a good instrument and it has a denominator bug — every ledger row is keyed by an `MVK_*` code, so a class A12 reports under any OTHER spelling can never appear in the residue, however reachable or valuable it is. It is not a small omission: the kernel declares **65 unprefixed classes** across its nine error-type enums, **49 of them the error-text parameter surface** that every rule's stored `errorMessage` meets ([KF323](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf323), derived and corrected in place the same day).

**That surface is measured on the kernel side and had no checker family — SHIPPED since, and the two paragraphs below are kept as the derivation that found it rather than as open work** *(closed 2026-09-14; the family is `MESSAGE_PARAMETER_REFERENCE` in [`Check.kt`](../consistency/src/commonMain/kotlin/io/github/mbackschat/a12/dm/consistency/Check.kt), landed with [KF335](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf335)/[KF336](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf336), and it took exactly the safe slice the next paragraph names — the plain form, every other shape falling through to silence)*. `RuleMessageParameterLawsTest`, `RuleMessageParameterLanguageLawsTest` and `RuleMessageAdmissionGateLawsTest` already hold grids over it — the membership gate that answers `INVALID_FIELD`, the quoting rule, the suffix forms, the semantic-index key. So it sits in the same bucket family 35 came from, which was the cheapest family so far: the kernel law already locked, only the portable half missing.

**It is deliberately NOT started here, and the reason is the convergence gate rather than its value.** A message parameter has many forms — a category suffix, a base-year terminal with a signed offset, a semantic-index key, an escaped `$$`, an exempt-terminal quoting rule — and each form this checker did not recognise would be reported as a bad field reference, which is an over-rejection, the one direction this project forbids. The safe slice is the plain `$Name$` / `$Name.value$` form with every other shape falling through to silence, and that slice needs its own acceptance matrix before it is opened rather than at the end of a long session.

**Re-derive the residue against BOTH vocabularies before picking the next family**, and read the tail-collision note in KF323 first: five unprefixed classes share their tail with an `MVK_` code, so a diff that strips prefixes merges laws that are not the same.

### C record — the thirty-eighth family: the circle a model's computations may not form (2026-09-13)

**This is the first family whose law is a GRAPH over the whole model rather than a property of one declaration**, and it was worth reaching for precisely because it looked relational and is not: the topological sort A12 runs reads only which field each computation writes and which fields its own members read, both of which a stored model spells out. Nothing about it needs a document, which is what moved it out of Order 6 and into reach ([KF334](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf334)).

**Two rows decided the implementation, and neither could have been reasoned to.** A read in an alternative's `precondition` builds an edge exactly like an operation read — the two members look nothing alike, and a field read is even spelled differently in each (`FieldFilled(Volume)` against `[Volume]`), so reading only `operation` is the natural implementation and would admit a model A12 refuses. And the two ends of a circle may sit in different groups, closed through an aggregate in one direction and an absolute read in the other, which is what licenses keying the graph on resolved paths alone.

**The pre-emption is the part a checker cannot infer.** A computation reading its own target is a cycle of length one in any graph an implementation would build, and A12 answers it with `MVK_ERROR_REFERENCE_TO_CALCULATED_FIELD`. The self-loop is therefore dropped inside the edge builder rather than filtered at the report, so nothing downstream can reintroduce it. This checker does not carry the self-read law — its two pre-emptions need derived-scale inference, a standing gap — so silence there is an under-report, which is safe; answering with the cycle class would not have been.

**The measurement cost was two wrong probes before a right one, and both were spelling.** A field read inside an operation is `[Weight]`, not `Weight`: the first probe returned `MVK_UNEXPECTED_TOKEN` on all four rows, which reads exactly like "the law does not fire here" and would have retired the candidate. Rendering one computation through the typed surface and printing what it produced settled it in one run — the estate's own DSL is the cheapest oracle for a spelling question, and guessing at one is how a grid measures the parser instead of the law.

### C record — the thirty-seventh family: the predicates a computation's precondition may not use (2026-09-13)

**The denominator was read from the Kernel's source, not from the ledger row that described it.** That row said "custom conditions, `RepetitionNotUnique`, or the legacy count-given op" and happened to be right; `NormalisierteBerechnungsFormHelper`'s own `if / else if / else if` chain is what settles it, and reading it also produced the fact the row could not have carried — that the third feature has no English spelling at all ([KF333](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf333)). Naming a subset as if it were the whole is the failure this estate keeps measuring, so the source read is the cheap half of the work, not the thorough-looking half.

**Two of three are reported and the third is a stated boundary.** The count-given op is a DE-only terminal with no catalog id, so an `en_US` model cannot reach that arm and this family has no measurement for it. Silence there is an under-report, which is always safe; a refusal with no measured basis is the one direction this project forbids, so the omission is recorded in the family's own KDoc rather than left to look like an oversight.

**The RULE control is what makes this a computation law.** Both predicates are ordinary in an `errorCondition` — measured — so a checker refusing them wherever it met them would refuse a legal model. This is the third consecutive family whose real content is a slot separation rather than a new shape, which is worth noticing: the stored members look alike by design, and every one of these families is one careless generalization from an over-rejection.

**The OPERATION row is the one that was nearly skipped, and it is the sharpest.** The precondition and the operation are two strings on the same alternative, read through the same code, and the filter keeping this family off the operation had no control until that row was measured. A12 answers the same predicate in an operation with `MVK_UNEXPECTED_TOKEN` — its *grammar* vocabulary — so judging both slots alike would have produced the wrong class, not merely a finding too many. The checker under-reports that shape (a `Name(path)` operation is well-formed to the grammar family, and what A12 refuses is the operator's KIND — the standing arity/operand-kind gap), which is disclosed in the mirror test rather than hidden behind an empty expectation.

**The candidate recorded as NEXT was then investigated and declined, which is the cheaper half of the enumeration.** `MVK_REF_ERROR_ENTITY_IF_FILLED_NOT_REFERENCED` and its `_IF_NOT_FILLED_` twin were carried as "an `IfFilled` marker in the error text must name an entity the condition references". The kernel source says otherwise on both counts: the law is about two declared LISTS of relative paths, not message text, and a stored rule carrying either member is **UNREADABLE** — A12's deserializer refuses the property, so no consistency verdict is reached at all ([KF61](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf61), amended in place). Both ledger rows are corrected. That is the third ledger row this week whose stated LOCUS was wrong, after the three index-family rows — a pattern worth naming: a row written from a code's NAME rather than from its firing site reads as a law and points at nothing.

**Two pieces of speculative code came out in the same pass.** `namesKeyword` re-tested each spelling against `CONDITION_KEYWORDS` after the caller had already resolved it from `CONDITION_OPERATOR_KEYWORDS`, whose values are a subset of that table — a branch no model can take, and a mutant relaxing it would have survived for want of a control. The family's `isEmpty()` early return was the same thing one layer up: both ids exist in both languages, so it was dead. Replaced with `getValue`, which fails loudly if the generated table ever stops carrying them.

### C record — the thirty-sixth family: the index field a computation may not decide, and how the next family was CHOSEN (2026-09-13)

**The family itself is small; the way it was selected is the part worth recording.** Browsing the ledger for a likely-looking code is how the previous two were picked, and it wasted a whole laws test earlier the same day on a law already locked. This one came from an enumeration instead: every `MVK_*` the ledger calls `kernel-delegated` (121), minus every code the checker's main source emits (71), leaving a printed residue to read (104). *(Figures re-derived 2026-09-14; they previously read 107 and 65, and the gap is the PREDICATE rather than growth — name it when you re-derive, or you will get a third pair. `kernel-delegated` counts a ledger ROW whose bucket cell CONTAINS that word, so a row bucketed `pre-checked + kernel-delegated` is in; `emits` counts a `"MVK_..."` string LITERAL anywhere in `consistency/src/commonMain`, so a code named only in KDoc is out while one listed in a withholding set is in.)* Most of it is relational (iteration, semantic index, parallel iteration — Order 6) or per-operator typing, which is the standing gap; the local declaration laws stand out immediately. Re-derive that diff rather than trusting this paragraph; it is two commands.

**`MVK_ERROR_INDEX_AS_CALCULATED_FIELD` had never been put to the Kernel from either estate.** The ledger carried the law as prose, no lock named the code, and the measurement confirmed it reachable — a real verdict a consumer receives, not a build refusal like the group-valued target beside it ([KF330](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf330)).

**The SORT field is the whole reason this is a separate family and not a line in the last one.** `indexFieldName` and `sortFields[].name` are the same kind of member on the same group, resolved by the same code — this checker reaches both through one shared helper — so an implementation that refused a computed index field would refuse a computed sort field in the same breath. A12 admits the second. Without that row the family is one careless edit from an over-rejection, and nothing would have caught it.

**Two scope questions were measured rather than inherited.** Whether the law follows the computation's own group or its resolved TARGET: it follows the target, so keying on the resolved field alone is correct. And whether the rule slot answers the same way: it does not, which is the same slot-separation the thirty-fourth family measured, holding on a second law.

**The control needed a control, which is the recurring shape.** The first outside-the-group row wrote into the field its own operation sums, so it drew the self-read class instead of the locus under test — a control moving two variables, caught only because its verdict named a code that had nothing to do with indexes.

### C record — the thirty-fifth family: what `Length` over a raw-type field may look like, and the parenthesis that decides it (2026-09-13)

**The kernel law was already measured and locked; only the checker half was missing.** [KF35](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf35) and `NvvRawTypeLawsTest` have carried the raw-type reads since 2026-07-15, so this family needed no new kernel work for its core — which is what made it the cheapest family available and worth picking over a code with no measurement behind it.

**One row had to be measured anyway, and it inverted the obvious implementation.** The lock fixes the ACCEPTED set — `Length(f) > c` and the mirror, as the whole rule condition. A family keying on "the whole condition is that, else report" asserts the stronger claim that every OTHER layout is refused, which an accepted set can never establish. The complement grid measured it: **redundant parentheses are TRANSPARENT**, so `(Length(f) > 5)` is ADMITTED and the token-exact match a reader reaches for first would refuse a legal model. Both comparison reversals and `Or` are refused as expected; `Not(…)` turns out to be a GRAMMAR refusal one tier earlier and belongs to no raw-type gate. The ordinary-string control carries every one of those layouts and stays admitted, which is the axis separating the layout from the field.

**Two gates, reported in A12's order.** The shape is decided first and pre-empts the constant domain, where a decimal point or a signed-32-bit overflow draws `MVK_INTERNAL_ERROR`. Reporting the constant on a text that also fails the shape would name a code A12 never reached — measured, and the reason the family does not simply validate the constant wherever it finds one.

**A stale ledger row fell out of it.** `MVK_INTERNAL_ERROR` was recorded as *"not author-triggerable as far as measured, and not a structural law"*, while a lock in this repository had been measuring exactly that trigger since before the row was written. Corrected in place. That is the second time this week a ledger disposition has disagreed with a green lock beside it, both found by needing the fact rather than by auditing the ledger.

**What it deliberately does not report.** A12 closes the other value windows on a raw type with `MVK_INVALID_RAW_TYPE` — a comparison, a value list, a computation's bare copy. Deciding those needs which operand POSITIONS of which operator are value positions, which is the per-operator catalog this plan does not reproduce. The class stays with the Kernel and is recorded rather than guessed at: silence under-reports, which is safe, and a guess would not be.

### C record — the thirty-fourth family: what a computation may compute INTO, and the twin slot that answers differently (2026-09-13)

**The family is a pair of measurements, not one.** A rule's `errorEntityRelPath` and a computation's `computedFieldRelPath` hold the same kind of value, are read by the same reader and resolve through the same code, so an implementation judges them alike unless a row says otherwise — and exactly one row does: a computation may not compute into a group, while a rule may report on one, fixed or repeatable alike ([KF329](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf329)). Either half alone licenses the wrong rule for the other slot, which is why the rule column is in the grid rather than cited from the family next door.

**The code it reports is NOT A12's key for the law, and the measurement is what decides that.** `MVK_GROUP_AS_CALCULATED_FIELD` exists, but the Kernel's builder refuses a group-valued computed field before `checkConsistency` runs, so no consumer ever sees that key from this shape. The report says `CK_MODEL_NOT_BUILDABLE` — the code the checker owns for precisely this tier, already carrying the computed field that names nothing. A family reaching for the `MVK_*` key because the law has one would have claimed a verdict A12 does not issue.

**It is another `erased` row that is live in stored DM-JSON**, the bucket that keeps producing families: the kits cannot author the shape because their computed-field slot is a typed field handle, and the checker reads a string where a group path is one edit away. The wildcard family landed from the same bucket the same day.

**The group KIND does not enter it.** Fixed and repeatable are refused alike, so the family needs no repetition reasoning — measured rather than assumed, because every other group law landed this week has turned on exactly that axis.

### The checker's standing over-rejection control, and what it found on its first run (2026-09-13)

**Thirty-three families had no control that could see a family firing on an ORDINARY model.** Each is locked against hand-written fixtures exercising the shape it judges, and a fixture written beside an implementation shares whatever that implementation assumes — which is exactly how the wildcard family's invented `repeatable` member passed every test it had. `adapter.laws.CheckerSilenceOnCleanModelsTest` supplies the missing direction: every committed model `BoundModel.ofJson` builds must draw no `A12_CONSTRAINT` ERROR from the checker. 40 of the 43 committed DM-JSON files qualify; the three that do not are include-bearing and need a resolver this sweep does not supply, so they drop out rather than needing an exclusion list.

**The Kernel picks the population, which is the property that makes the sweep maintainable.** Nothing here decides whether a model is valid — a deliberately broken fixture simply fails to build and is not measured — so adding a fixture never requires touching this test. A floor of 15 answers "did the sweep reach real models", deliberately far below the live 40 so it does not move as the estate grows.

**It found a real over-rejection on its first run, in the oldest family in the module.** `CustomCondition <Name>` names host code the consuming system registered ([KERNEL-SEMANTICS §14](KERNEL-SEMANTICS.md)), and the reference scanner resolved that name as an entity — so every model using the escape hatch drew `MVK_INVALID_ENTITY`, and three committed ones do. The shape parser already knew the form (`applikationsBedingung`: a callable-condition keyword followed by a bare NAME rather than a call); the two readers had simply read the grammar differently, which is the class a single-reader estate cannot produce and a two-reader one produces silently. The fix makes the reference scan apply the parser's own test.

**A corpus arm was added the same day, and it is the half that matters.** The committed models were authored here, against this checker's own reading of A12 — a shared origin no number of them removes. The reference corpus (`a12-kernel-mm` — all 59 stored models, Kernel-expanded then Kernel-built, the same population the parser sweep holds) was written by A12's own team, and it immediately produced a row our grids structurally could not: `MVK_ERROR_FIELD_NOT_REFERENCED` on two rules whose condition pairs the `RuleGroup` self-reference with an ordinary operand. Every self-reference row we held carried the spelling ALONE, so "the self-reference reaches the rule's whole group" and "a condition naming no entity has nothing to compare" predicted the same verdict on all of them — the constant-across-the-set confound, and the corpus is what sat on the other side of the axis ([KF324](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf324), corrected in place).

**One of the eleven findings on that first corpus run was a regression this session had introduced minutes earlier**, which is the sharpest evidence for the control. The `CustomCondition` fix above had been written to reuse the SHAPE parser's test — any callable-condition keyword not followed by `(` takes the bare name behind it. That test is sound in the parser and unsound in the reference scan, because the parser applies it only where a condition is expected positionally while the scan is a flat token walk: the UNCATALOGED infix `In` swallowed its right operand, and nine rules across five models reported a phantom absolute reference. The committed arm was green throughout. Reusing a proved mechanism across a boundary is a claim, not an inheritance — and the tell was exactly the documented one, that the reuse needed no new branch.

**What the sweep did NOT reach, and the second arm that now does (closed 2026-09-13).** Every corpus model is handed to the checker already EXPANDED by the Kernel, so the checker's own include resolution — its `ConsistencyWorkspace` mount path, the one a TypeScript or browser consumer has no Kernel to stand in for — never ran against a real model; only the own-domain `WorkspaceMountTest` covered it, and the committed include-bearing models dropped out of the committed arm as well, because `BoundModel.ofJson` is called there without a resolver. `noFamilyReportsWhenTheCheckerMountsTheIncludesItself` closes it: the UNEXPANDED entry plus the sources its `header.modelReferences` name go through `ConsistencyWorkspace`, with the Kernel still picking the population from the expanded form.

**Its vacuity trap is the sharpest one this control has met, and the obvious guard does not work.** An unexpanded mount returns a report with no findings, which is indistinguishable from silence — and the STATUS cannot separate them, because a clean report is `Inconclusive` too (coverage stays incomplete while required families are unimplemented). A first version asserted on the status and failed on both models for that reason, which is the useful direction to fail in. `checksCompleted` is the discriminator: empty on that early return alone. A mutant that hands the workspace an empty provider map is caught by this arm.

**The population is two models and cannot be widened from the reference corpus — measured, not assumed (2026-09-13).** All 59 `a12-kernel-mm` document models are self-contained: not one declares a `header.modelReferences` entry, so the corpus that gives the other arms their independence contributes nothing at all to this one. The two committed include-bearing models are the whole available population, and they cover both purposes (`include` and `typeDefinitions`) with one real group mount between them. Widening it means authoring own-domain multi-model fixtures, not finding more real ones.

**The lane is scoped to that one class, not to `:adapter:test`.** It reads three committed model trees as FILES, so they are its declared inputs, and the broad lane excludes it — the pattern TESTING-SPEC requires for any test that reads repo files at runtime. `test` still depends on it, so a full run and CI drive it unchanged.

### C record — the thirty-third family: a repetition wildcard on something with no repetitions, and a DM-JSON member that does not exist (2026-09-13)

**The first family that judges a REFERENCE rather than an operand, and the measurement is what licenses that.** On a REPEATABLE group the same star draws three different answers by position — `MVK_NO_WILDCARDS_ALLOWED` in a scalar slot, `MVK_NO_WILDCARDS_G_ALLOWED` in a quantifier's operand list, admitted in a count or an aggregate — so a family that ignores position is exactly the shape that should be wrong. On a non-repeatable one every position collapses to `MVK_INVALID_WILDCARD` ([KF328](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf328)): eight positions across two carriers, each beside its repeatable control. Per-position independence is what a reference-level reading assumes, so it was measured rather than inherited from the two positions that motivated the family.

**It outruns the entity gate, which is the opposite order from every other pair measured in this cluster.** A starred fixed group followed by a leaf that resolves to nothing draws this class and says nothing about the leaf — so the family does not wait for the whole reference to resolve; it resolves each starred SEGMENT's own prefix and judges that. Every other law landed here pre-empts on an unresolvable operand instead.

**The defect this family produced is the worst kind available in this work: an invented DM-JSON member, agreed on by the implementation and by its own evidence.** The repetition test read `"repeatable": true`. A12 has no such member — it is `repeatability`, an integer whose default is 1, and `ModelDocument` tests `> 1`. Every test passed, because the hand-written fixtures beside the implementation carried the same invention, so the two agreed with each other and not with A12. On a real model the family would have reported `MVK_INVALID_WILDCARD` for every CORRECTLY starred group — the over-rejection direction this project forbids, reached while implementing a family whose entire purpose is to catch a wrong star. Nothing in the family's own tests could have caught it; it went red only when the family was wired in and a fixture written for something else disagreed.

**So the guard that follows DERIVES the accepted spelling instead of listing it.** `FixtureRepetitionMemberGuardTest` drafts a repeatable group through the real emitter, reads back which member carries the count, and then requires every repetition-shaped member name in the checker's sources to be exactly that one — `maxRepetitions` and any other plausible twin fail without anyone enumerating them, and a rename in the kits moves the guard with it. The asymmetry it closes is structural rather than careless: the law fixtures are drafted through `DocumentModelDraft` and the unit fixtures are hand-written JSON, and only an oracle keeps those two spellings together.

**Extended 2026-09-13 — the two shapes it left alone were then MEASURED, and the family names all three classes.** The first pass declined them because A12's ledger gives each its own code and naming the wrong class on a correct rejection is still a wrong finding. One grid settled both: the rule-context entity draws `MVK_INVALID_WILDCARD_FOR_RULEGROUP` and a leading up-step draws `MVK_INVALID_WILDCARD_REL` ([KF328](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf328), extended in place). Two of its rows are pre-emptions wearing a measurement's clothes — from a root-level rule the DEPTH class answers first, and a mid-path `..` is refused by the parser, starred or not — and each carries the unstarred control that says so, which is what keeps them out of the wildcard column.

**Each of the two new classes needed a question the reference list cannot answer, and for the same reason.** The rule-context spelling is a KEYWORD, so a starred one never appears as a reference at all and the family asks the condition TEXT; a reference that is nothing but up-steps names no entity, so it is not reported as a reference either — that one stays a disclosed under-report rather than a second text-level question. This is the third family in two days whose law turns on a spelling `referencesIn` deliberately drops, which is a property of that reader worth naming rather than rediscovering.

**And the grid exposed a parser over-rejection in passing.** `GroupFilled(..*)` is refused by A12 SEMANTICALLY, so it parses — while the checker's parser took `..` and `*` as a product, which fails outright with nothing after the star and, worse, *succeeds* with a path after it. The two readers therefore disagreed about the same text in both directions. The path production now takes a wildcard after an up-step; the 657-condition corpus sweep is unchanged, as a strictly looser parser must be.

**One shape is still left alone**: a star on a named turning point (`..Ancestor*`), whose class is unmeasured. Silence is the answer where the class is in doubt, because a wrong code on a correct rejection is indistinguishable to a reader from a wrong rejection.

### C record — the thirty-second family: a FIELD where a group predicate refuses one, and an ERASURE's reason found false (2026-09-13)

**The family's content is which carriers do NOT have the gate.** `MVK_NO_FIELDS_ALLOWED` reads like a law about group predicates, and five of the eight carriers admit a field operand outright: only `NumberOfFilledGroups` and the two scalar presence predicates refuse one ([KF327](KERNEL-FINDINGS.md#kf327)). A family assembled from the operators' names would have refused five legal shapes — the same failure the enum-value-token family met one week earlier, and the reason a carrier set is measured rather than derived.

**It also corrected a Java-side erasure's stated reason, which is the `erased` bucket's characteristic failure.** The ledger recorded the typed `GroupRef`/`FieldRef` split as resting on "group predicates take groups only". That sentence is false, so the erasure had no basis; it now carries the measurement instead, and stands, because the split bites on the AUTHORING path alone — no read path reconstructs typed operands from a stored condition, so a model carrying the shape stays readable. That is the sharper half of the client-side-refusal rule, checked rather than assumed.

**Three laws now share one call and one pass, each with its own carrier set and none a subset of another.** Duplicate and overlap run over all six group-list carriers, the root law over those six split three-three, and the kind law over a set of three that includes one group-list carrier and two scalars the others never see. They are ordered because A12 orders them, and the order was measured end to end rather than composed from the individual findings.

### C record — the thirty-first family: a ROOT group where the carrier does not admit one (2026-09-13)

**It rides the thirtieth family's capability and lands the day after it for that reason.** The law is per call and per operand, so it was unreachable before the argument list was; once [`callsIn`] existed the whole of KF185's root half became implementable, and the measurement that had been deferred as "needs operator identity" took one grid.

**The carriers split six ways into two threes, and the split does not follow their names.** `AllGroupsFilled`, `NotAllGroupsFilled` and `GroupsNotCollectivelyFilled` refuse a root operand outright, sole or not; `AtLeastOneGroupFilled`, `NoGroupFilled` and `NumberOfFilledGroups` admit a sole root and refuse one beside anything else. Two of those six had never been put through the gate, and `NoGroupFilled` — whose name pairs it with the refusing three — sits with the admitting three.

**The wildcard asymmetry is the trap, and it runs against the family landed the day before.** A starred operand is taken OUT of the duplicate comparison and left fully inside the root gate; an implementation that reused the skip would admit `AllGroupsFilled(/Batches*, Billing)`, which A12 refuses. The two laws share an operand list, a file and a call, and disagree about the same token.

**The locus question was the one that decided whether this family was implementable at all, and the answer is that it does not arise.** An unstarred repeatable co-operand makes A12 report its wildcard class from the outside anchoring and the root class from the inside — so the root violation holds in both and only the reporting ORDER moves.
